And it came to pass, as they were burying a man, that, behold, they spied a band of men; and they cast the man into the sepulchre of Elisha: and when the man was let down, and touched the bones of Elisha, he revived, and stood up on his feet.” If it may appear probable that, in the remotest ages, men were chiefly given to prophecy and to inner perceptions, in our day and among us it is woman who most frequently possesses these gifts: nevertheless, the Bible also records many examples of women prophetesses, as, for example, the woman of Endor, who possessed the spirit of divination and whom Saul went to consult; Huldah the prophetess (2 Kings 22, 14), and Deborah, the wife of Lapidoth, etc.

## A Retrospective Glance: Israel and the Heathen Nations

Let us once more cast a retrospective glance upon the people of Israel, according to the history of the Old Testament, and upon the ancient days of the Orientals, and compare the magic which existed among them with that of later years, and we shall find many and essential differences. In the first place, I have already remarked how the people of Israel stood alone and unique before all the heathen nations, and how magic took among them an essential and diversified form. For although the Jews remained so long in Egypt, they brought back with them on their return very little of Egyptian magic; that is to say, of that true theurgic magical art which is the product of natural powers and human inventions. Their magical ecstasies and their wonderful operations partook more of divine inspiration, while the black art, practised by natural means and intended to produce supernatural effects, was visited with severe punishments as an impious work of witchcraft and sorcery. We find quite the contrary in heathenism, since there the true knowledge of the divine was entirely wanting, or else it was adulterated by traditions and obscured by mysteries. Thus, in Egypt, Athor — according to the Theogony of Hesiod, the darkness of night — was worshipped as the great unknown, by a profound silence; but to the Israelites the light appeared in the unity of God, whom they worshipped with resounding hymns. Throughout the whole ancient world of heathenism reigned the power of the principle of nature, which lowered the spiritual vibration to the level of the earthly. The true magic of the divine was hidden from this wandering people, covered with a veil through which only a few gleams penetrated. The light shone in the darkness, but the darkness comprehended it not. Hamberger says: “Heathenism was capable of gathering only isolated, as it were oblique, rays; but the elect of God, the posterity of Shem, in whom humanity was to be represented as such, and to whom all the rest of mankind, as mere common people (Gojim), was subordinate, could rejoice in an influence not merely indirect, but central, on the part of the Lord.” “Thou art an holy people unto the Lord, saith God (Deut. 7, 6). The Lord thy God hath chosen thee to be a special people unto Himself, above all people that are upon the face of the earth.”

Israel was called to this, not so much to conceive and grasp the outward glory of God, the ideal world, but much rather His inner being — to be led ever deeper into the sanctuary of the divine personality. This, however, cannot be done at a single stroke; and if Israel is not the only one pardoned and favored, but if through it all the nations of the earth are to be blessed, this could be accomplished only gradually and by degrees. Madame Schlegel says: “An aspiration, a love, is the root and beginning of all higher and divine knowledge. Perseverance in seeking, in faith and in the battle of life, prefigures only the middle of the way; the end must always remain something hoped for. The necessary period of preparation for this gradual process can be neither overturned nor set aside by the noblest efforts of man. The character and the very history of the Hebrew nations are greatly misunderstood, for the precise reason that these things are not duly observed. The whole existence of this people was built upon hope alone, and the supreme center of its inner life was placed at a great distance in the future. Upon this rests also the principal difference in the method of the holy deliverance of the Hebrews, as it appears among the other ancient Asiatic nations. In the old monuments of those nations, their gaze, in the properly historical parts, is directed rather toward the glorious past, with a feeling of regret for what man has since lost. From all the fullness of these holy and touching memories, and of the most ancient common proverbs, Moses, in his most direct and most fixed revelations for the Hebrew children, according to the wisest law of economy, drew but very little, and only that which appeared the most indispensable and most necessary for his people and for God’s design regarding them. And just as all the writings, from the first lawgiver — who, for this sole purpose, led and brought his favored people out of the Egyptian bondage in a spiritual sense and according to the whole mode of thought — down to the time of the prophet-king and singer of the psalms, down to the last voice of warning and of promise which died away in the wilderness, are, as to outward content and inner understanding, in conformity with the prophetic writings: so the people itself may be called a prophetic people in the highest sense of the word; and it really is so, taken historically, in the whole course of the world and in its wonderful destinies: it was so and it is so.”

Molitor says further: “The guidance of the Israelites furnishes the most manifest proof of the divine nature of religion. Among all nations there are oracles; in all important affairs and transactions they are consulted, and nothing is undertaken in life without having first obtained the favorable judgment of the gods. Yet these oracles do not appear to be positive guides of the people. They confine themselves to answering the questions put to them. In no heathen religion, consequently, do we observe a really positive, sensible and divine guidance. There man stands solely in his own strength. The case is entirely different with the people of Israel, which is nothing and has nothing of itself, but whose whole existence and whole guidance are solely the work of the Divinity. Where is a nation to be found which has had such an ethical guidance? Where do you find a people which has made humility, obedience and a filial surrender to God its first and principal duty, which has accepted chastisement as a pledge of love, and which has been led to its destination through adversity and humiliation? It is true that there are also guidances and trials in heathenism, but they are trials only in the vigorous mastery of evil and the courageous bearing of great burdens. Nowhere, consequently, do we find merit or praise for abasement or humility. When, for example, it was said of Moses: ‘He was a meek man,’ it was a praise which was never bestowed upon a heathen hero.” (Kabbalah, part 3, page 116.)

The Hebrew prophets and the heathen soothsayers present an essential difference.

Even if the natural ground of magical visions appears here as elsewhere; if imagination and sympathy, if the outward influences of nature often produced similar effects, and if the Israelites had learned and brought back from Egypt many and various secrets — as is amply proved, in the Old Testament, by the school of the prophets of Samuel, and as the holy and inspired dance, the prophetic song, etc., had in them something contagious, so that the disciples of the prophets were seized and overpowered by it, as happened to Saul, who fell in among them and prophesied, so that the proverb: “Is Saul also among the prophets?” still attests something very curious and surprising — nevertheless, with regard to motive and effect, great differences are discovered, which well deserve to be set forth here. They are as follows:

1. The magician, the Brahman of India, the mystic priest, etc., put themselves into a state of ecstasy and into a pretended union with God by methods of their own choosing; Moses and the true prophets of Israel receive, to serve Him, an unexpected call.

2. The magician raises himself, by his innate strength, to a state and condition superior to those of the world around him; he isolates himself by design, and his isolation becomes imperative; hence proceed the various castes and the various degrees of life — such, for example, as the Indian and Egyptian castes — which exercise a peculiar influence upon the so diverse conditions of earthly life and of intelligence. Moses and the prophets are simpler: it is in the passive awe of a profound solitude that they suddenly hear the call, and they follow it with humility, with veiled face. The deliverance of his people Moses does not effect by a mighty will, and he claims no precedence; he does not divide the people into different castes, but he separates the organic unity of this people, which he consecrates to God, from heathen blindness; he is himself the interpreter of faith in the sovereign providence of God, of the fear of punishments to come, of the love of God, as well as of order and justice among men.

3. Contempt of the world and pride in his own dignity and knowledge characterize the magical seer. A wise use of life, obedience in the service of God, and the constant remembrance of his sinful weakness lead the prophet to implore divine help, to pray to know the truth, to accomplish not his own will but a higher will. For the Brahman, for example, the earth is a hell, a place of torment; for the prophet it is a school in which, in the fulfilment of duty, the peace of true happiness may be found.

4. The magician is a lawgiver; the prophets are obedient disciples, who preach and explain the revelations of God.

5. There, the means of falling into ecstasy are found: self-renunciation and the unnatural mortification of the body. Here, the world is disposed for the most judicious enjoyment of life. The prophet needs no extraordinary means to enter into ecstasy; he utters the immediate word of God without preparation and without mortifying his body, he presents it, and he lives among his fellow men.

6. The very content of the prophecy, in the highest ecstasies of the magicians, is a kind of luminous radiance; when they are plunged in it, the world with its signatures, and perhaps also the inner state of the mind, may become clearly visible to them, after the manner of the clairvoyants of our day; but their lips remain sealed in this ecstatic state of bliss and in the dazzling brilliance of a pathological self-illumination. Hence those numerous antagonistic figures of truth and illusion, of sensational emotions and phantasmagorias of broken and discordant forms, of convulsions and contortions of body and soul, which we find again in our mesmeric subjects. Their visions, like those of certain somnambulists, are not worthy of belief and cannot, within their own frame, be understood without a previous explanation. With the prophets, the visions, as to form, are the illumination of a gentle divine light, reflected by the mirror of a pure spirit, which preserves a perfect personality and awakens a feeling of dependence upon God and the outer world. The content of their visions relates to the general interests of life, in matters of religion and of the state; the prophet speaks, and his words are true doctrines, clearly enunciated for the good of all men and all ages, and understood by all. He seeks happiness, but he does not find it in penance: he finds it in his vocation, in the diffusion of the word of God, not in secret contemplations, but in communicating it to his fellow men by active co-operation. The true prophet, consequently, does not lose himself in inner contemplations, any more than he forgets himself in the world; he remains in living relation with God and with his neighbor, in word and in deed. Lastly,

7. Since the motives and the processes differ essentially among the inspired races, their aims and their results differ likewise. The Indian magician laments over the gradual degradation of the spiritual, fallen from its original splendor, through the rapidly succeeding ages of the world, down into perishable nature and into the kingdom of death, and he deplores the misery attached thereto: the discontent, the confusion and the rending of the spirit, all of which we may find again among the various heathen nations. On the other hand, how greatly universal brotherhood and the community of men have grown, step by step, through the agency of the true prophet, and how greatly the spiritual has been glorified! The spirit which hovered over the Jewish religion spread its peaceful influences in the West ever farther and wider; and while, in the first case, everything is lost in weakness and darkness, in the second, mountains are moved by active faith, and trees are planted by mutual help and counsel, trees whose fruit will ripen for enjoyment only in another world, toward which we should turn our eyes and our efforts.

The aim of the magician’s life is to secure for himself an inner contemplation full of delights; the true prophet lives by faith and not by sight.

Historians and philosophers of modern times have declared that the ecstatic visions of the prophets of Israel and those of the apostles were identical with magnetic phenomena. In order to form a clearer judgment upon the circumstances set forth above, we shall add the following:

The true prophets receive from God an extraordinary call, and they are urged by the Holy Spirit to proclaim the will and design of God. They are called seers, men of God, servants and messengers of the Lord, angels and watchmen. The marks of the true prophets of the Old Testament were: 1st, that their prophecies agreed with the doctrines of Moses and the patriarchs (Deuteronomy 13, 1); 2nd, that their prophecies were fulfilled (Deuteronomy 18, 21; Jeremiah 28, 9); 3rd, that they wrought miracles, but only when a particular covenant was concluded or a particular reformation was to be undertaken; 4th, that they agreed with the other prophets (Isaiah 8, 2; Jeremiah 26, 18); 5th, that they led irreproachable lives (Jeremiah 27, 4; Micah 2, 11) and displayed a holy zeal in the work of God (Jeremiah 26, 13); and 6th, that they possessed great power of speech (Jeremiah 23, 28-29). Their office consisted in this: 1st, they instructed the people when the priests, upon whom this duty devolved, became indifferent; 2nd, they restored the worship of God when relaxed and fallen into decay (2 Kings 17, 18; Ezekiel 3, 17); 3rd, they foretold future events and, to this end, consulted God (1 Kings 14, 2-3; Ezekiel 22, 6-8); 4th, they prayed for the people and thus averted impending judgments (Genesis 20, 7; 2 Kings 19, 2); and 5th, they wrote down the will of God (1 Chronicles 29, 9 *[sic; read 29, 29]*).

The same may be said of the apostles and preachers of the living word. They were called ministers because Christ himself had chosen them and sent them to the ends of the earth to proclaim redemption and gather together his elect. They did not thrust themselves of their own accord into his service: Christ called them directly and taught them personally to proclaim the coming of the Messiah and, furnished with these credentials, to work wonders by the divine word. This new doctrine proceeded expressly from the prophets of the old covenant: repent and believe the gospel of redemption, and prove thereby that you love God above all things and your neighbor as yourselves. Their lives proved that they truly walked in the footsteps of their Lord and Master, in word and in deed, in works as in suffering.

If we hold fast to these characteristics in forming our judgment, it will not be difficult to distinguish between magnetic sight and prophetic inspiration, neither to esteem the former too highly nor to underestimate the latter. For if their superficial appearance seems at first identical, their difference soon becomes manifest when they are examined from a threefold point of view, namely: the cause, the content or form, and the intention.

According to the cause, the palpable difference consists in this: magical and magnetic second sight is chiefly the work of man; it grows upon a diseased physical soil, whether it be developed by the artifices of the physician or unfolds by chance. An abnormal state of health always precedes it, and the slumber of the outer senses is its first condition. If in one person this mesmeric disposition is greater than in another, it is because the physiological foundation lies in his own body; and if occasional circumstances favor second sight in others, these causes belong to the kingdom of nature, which holds the clairvoyant in powerful bonds and which remains his determining condition, even were he to attain the highest degree.

Prophetic inspiration is not a begetting of nature or of man: it is an emanation of the Holy Spirit and a divine decree. The divine call comes unexpectedly, and the physical condition has no relation to it. The physical powers can never become the determining powers; they remain dependent upon the spirit, which makes of them a means in the service of spiritual ends. Here there occurs neither mesmeric life with altered functions of the senses, nor physical crisis.

Secondly, according to the form, magnetic second sight depends directly upon the health and life of the seer, or rather it predominates in the relative modes of earthly life. The clairvoyant directs his attention at will toward objects of his own choosing, at least in the majority of cases, or else he interprets his own visions, conducts his own affairs or those of his immediate surroundings, or again he allows himself to be determined from without, without active and persevering independence, and without activity for the common good. Purely human nature, affectation and inclination are never wanting in the magic circle of the seer, and the operation of his will and his faith produces no supernatural or lasting effect, either upon himself or upon others.

The true prophet, as to form, knows not the diversity of visions, but an immutable content of scriptural work: the announcement of Him who is the Beginning and the End, and by whom all things were created. A prophet is not merely a seer: he is the organ of the divine will. To teach the true knowledge of God and to extend His kingdom, which is truth and love, such is his sole and constant occupation; wherefore he combats error and wickedness, in order to overcome the world. What is worldly or changeable — bigotry or sensuality, health, riches and the honors of the world, dominion over his fellow men — is not his concern. He does not preach a present happiness, but a future state of bliss, true peace in God and the hope of eternal life in His presence; not from personal impulse, from selfishness or from human considerations, but as a willing instrument of a perpetual illumination, inspired by God himself — a worthy example in work and in conduct; an obedient servant and mediator between God and man, between time and eternity, between heaven and earth. By prayer, in word and in deed, the prophet remains in living relation with God and with his fellow men.

The true prophets do not isolate themselves, nor do they sink into the absorbing depths of their own visions, feelings and relations. Their prophecies do not relate to personalities, but to the destiny of nations and of the world; wherefore they are able to manifest in their works supernatural powers, strengthened by the all-powerful might of their faith and will, and this power they exercise upon their own bodies as well as upon the bodies of others and upon all nature within its vast temporal limits. Sudden conversions and changes of opinion, the instantaneous healing of grave and inveterate diseases, warnings against threatening dangers, help brought from afar to the needy, consolation and strength given in trouble and suffering, etc., are so many proofs of their divine powers.

As we have already said, the aim of the true prophets was the revelation of the divine word to men, the extension of the kingdom of God upon earth, the ennoblement and welfare of the human race. Impelled by the spirit of God, upon whose assistance they relied, their efforts aimed at nothing less than to spread the light of truth, to inspire their fellow men with courage to combat evil, to awaken mutual attention and assistance, to extend peace and happiness among men. The self-exaltation and personal advantage of these organs of the Divinity did not come into consideration. The foundation of all their works was faith in the power of God, and they fulfilled the whole sum of the commandments through love, the noblest of all virtues: “but the fruit of the Spirit is love” (Galatians 5, 22); “and God giveth wisdom to them that love Him” (Ecclesiasticus 1, 10); “and his banner over me was love” (Song of Songs 2, 4).

Of these manifest variations the children of Israel bear a particular testimony: 1st, that the causes of the inner visions were really objective, and that there exists, outside the human intelligence, something which governs and rules the world of man, and that independently of the inner center of the spirit, while the peripheral senses of day and of nature are either inactive or in a very subordinate condition; 2nd, that there exists a still higher spiritual region, which positively lays hold of the reason and offers revelations which are not of a natural order, which cannot exist in these lower regions, and which are not mere fancies, illusions or hallucinations of an abnormal state of the brain: “The hand of the Lord was upon them”; 3rd, that the misapprehension, or rather the denial, which sophistical rationalism opposes, especially with regard to the Israelites, likewise represents things only superficially, just as does the pantheistic philosophy of nature, which distils everything into a common mass and sees in the prophets and saints only somnambulistic sects, placed upon a plane somewhat higher than that assigned to them in the dark and partial ages of telluric life; 4th, that, although this class of visions presents an outward resemblance to magical and magnetic visions, both according to the anthropological expression and according to the symbolical representations, as we have already seen, and must remind us of magnetic relations — inasmuch as prophetic revelation agrees with the purest forms of second sight, and the visions of dreams and divination arise from circumstances and conditions — we may find in the old covenant many preparations and dispositions, such as we have learned to know among the other magicians of the East. We often find also, among the prophets, that they withdrew into solitary places, that they fasted and gave themselves up to calm contemplations. Like the clairvoyants, they speak of a higher inner light, of a light which enlightens them, and they acknowledge that this higher illumination is the spirit of the Lord, whose hand came upon them and transfigured them; and they walked, as the Psalmist says, in the light of His countenance: “For in Thy light shall we see light; the Lord my God will enlighten my darkness. Thou art the living fountain, and in thy light we see light” (Psalms 36, 10; 18, 29).

The prophets describe the high divine light as an instantaneous sight, presented very frequently under the most familiar symbols, of which the vision of Daniel, by the side of the great river Hiddekel, is one of the most remarkable and will serve here as an illustration (Daniel 10, 2-21): “In those days I Daniel was mourning three full weeks. I ate no pleasant bread, neither came flesh nor wine in my mouth, neither did I anoint myself at all, till three whole weeks were fulfilled. Then I lifted up mine eyes, and looked, and behold a certain man clothed in linen, whose loins were girded with fine gold of Uphaz. His body also was like the beryl, and his face as the appearance of lightning, and his eyes as lamps of fire, and his arms and his feet like in color to polished brass, and the voice of his words like the voice of a multitude. And I Daniel alone saw the vision: for the men that were with me saw not the vision; but a great quaking fell upon them, so that they fled to hide themselves. Therefore I was left alone, and saw this great vision, and there remained no strength in me. Yet heard I the voice of his words: and when I heard the voice of his words, then was I in a deep sleep on my face, with my face toward the ground. And, behold, an hand touched me, which set me upon my knees and upon the palms of my hands. And he said unto me: O Daniel, a man greatly beloved, understand the words that I speak unto thee, and stand upright: for unto thee am I now sent. And when he had spoken this word unto me, I stood trembling. Then said he unto me: Fear not, Daniel: for from the first day that thou didst set thine heart to understand, and to chasten thyself before thy God, thy words were heard, and I am come for thy words. And when he had spoken such words unto me, I set my face toward the ground, and I became dumb. And, behold, one like the similitude of the sons of men touched my lips: then I opened my mouth, and spake, and said unto him that stood before me: O my lord, by the vision my sorrows are turned upon me, and I have retained no strength. For how can the servant of this my lord talk with this my lord? For as for me, straightway there remained no strength in me, neither is there breath left in me. Then there came again and touched me one like the appearance of a man, and he strengthened me, and said: O man greatly beloved, fear not: peace be unto thee! Be strong, yea, be strong. And when he had spoken unto me, I was strengthened, and said: Let my lord speak; for thou hast strengthened me. Then said he: Knowest thou wherefore I come unto thee? I will show thee that which is noted in the scripture of truth, etc.” The following passage from Passavant will serve as an elucidation of this vision:

“Such a state, and so penetrating an illumination of the spirit, can find their accord only in the original relation of the creature to the Creator. The created spirit does not generally exist for itself, nor by itself, but only in relation to the absolute being. The more perfect the creature, the more intimate and free is the communion between the creature and the Creator — the more is man a free agent and a co-worker with God. What holds for human nature and for every spiritual power, in perception as in accomplishment, holds also quite especially in the region where the human spirit, freed from earthly nature and from the bonds of time and place, is more active. As we have therefore admitted that the highest magical operation is that in which the human spirit becomes the divine organ, we are justified in admitting that the highest magical knowledge must be a divinely illuminated power of second sight, a spiritual contemplation moved and guided by the divine spirit. Inasmuch as we must regard the most intimate communion of the creature with the Creator as the final destiny of created spirits, we may regard the holy power of prophecy as the anticipation of a higher and more perfect condition, in which man knows even as he is known (1 Corinthians 13, 9 *[sic; read 13, 12]*), where his spiritual freedom will have attained a knowledge which earthly laws can no longer circumscribe. But since man must rise toward the good as well as receive it, this law of the free and created spirit will repeat itself, for man can rise, in various degrees, to a higher order even in this world, and be enlightened in the same measure. Taken in this connection, the power of the divine seer must not be considered separately from the other spiritual powers by which he can overcome all that is foreign to his nature, but rather as a fixed form, a power of the normal and regenerate soul. The spirit of man, the image of God, may become the mirror in which the divine type is reflected, in exact proportion to the purity of that image.” (Passavant, *Magnetic Life*, second edition, p. 167.)

It will then only be necessary to remark, in a general way, that God made use only of the nobility of the spirit of Israel for the education and redemption of the human family, and that this people, which had attached itself to heathen idolatry and was disobedient and rebellious, could be led only slowly to its true destiny, in the hard school of adversity and by heavy chastisements. The road from Ur of the Chaldees to Canaan, which the patriarch Abraham followed — how far it stretched, in length and in breadth, into Egypt, and from there, through the wilderness, to the promised land of which they were to take possession! Yes, the children of Israel were compelled to wander to and fro in the wilderness for forty years, because of their wavering between the service of God and heathen idolatry; they were sent into captivity to Babylon; the holy city had to be destroyed; and finally they had to endure the full measure of misfortune and persecution, and all this for having wavered between the service of the true God and the idolatry of the heathen. If, then, Israel is, as it is represented, the favored people of God, it can be nothing less than the pearl of perfection — and consequently also the mirror of perversity, which ever seeks after outward forms and ceremonies, and seeks happiness in nature and in the dissipations of the present: a happiness which cannot be found upon earth. The divine word of revelation alone can unveil the happiness of the peace and bliss of Paradise, and, in order to share in it, the spirit of man must accustom itself to two great qualifications, namely: natural obedience to the law, and a more than natural hope of attaining its final destiny beyond earthly life. In order to teach the children of Israel humble obedience to the laws, they were exposed to the severest trials and subjected to the vilest slavery.

It was to this people and to no other that the commandments were communicated in accents of thunder, by divinely appointed leaders, that it might keep them in the inner depths of the spirit, and not merely superficially, by the outer senses. The sacrifices and feasts were not to serve as temporal occasions of rejoicing: they were to serve as a typical veil, through which the true light of the coming Messiah might be perceived, as the flower bud turns toward the approaching light of the sun. The Mercy Seat, the Cherubim, the Holy of Holies, the Pillar of Fire and the Temple of Solomon are so many symbolical manifestations issuing from magical visions, and which announce the coming of Christ. That the whole Mosaic ordinance was symbolical and hieroglyphic, every connoisseur admits, and the following words clearly express this fact: “And look that thou make them after their pattern, which was showed thee in the mount.” Moses, the man of God, therefore constitutes, in the history of the children of Israel, the second period of the beginning of religious development. The forms and ceremonies of the law were strictly imposed, from that time forward, only to impress upon men the importance of the revealed word. But what a long period elapsed between the wanderings and sufferings of the Israelites, the wonders with which they were surrounded, the death of the first-born in Egypt, the lightnings which flashed from the heights of Mount Sinai, and the time of King David, with whom the third period began! “He was ruddy, and withal of a beautiful countenance, and goodly to look to” (1 Samuel 16, 12), and he, the shepherd of his father’s sheep, was chosen by the Lord to be king over His people. His obedience toward God and his unshakable faith not only caused it to be said of him “that he was a man after God’s own heart”: root of Judah, born in Bethlehem, he became also the figure of Christ. He was at once king and prophet, and he had to bear many sorrows and trials. A servant of God, he sought to lead the children of Israel toward God at Jerusalem, the mountain of peace, where at last the gentle and illuminating light of the divine Prince of Peace appeared to the world, coming forth from the dark and transitory night, upon the cross.

“Now I say, that the heir, as long as he is a child, differeth nothing from a servant, though he be lord of all; but is under tutors and governors until the time appointed of the father. Even so we, when we were children, were in bondage under the elements of the world.” (Galatians 4, 1-3.)

“But when the fulness of the time was come, God sent forth his Son, made of a woman, made under the law, to redeem them that were under the law, that we might receive the adoption of sons.” (Galatians 4, 4-5.)

The coming of Christ upon earth was not an accidental event, as other natural events were. His coming was a revelation ordained by God from the foundation of the world. I have already shown, in my anthropological views, that Christ, if he was really the Son of God, must necessarily appear at a fixed time and place, that his appearance would constitute the beginning of the second period of human power, and that this event would take place on the western coast of Asia.
## The New Covenant

Having set forth many of the most extraordinary events of the old covenant which bear upon magic and magnetism, it appears important, on more than one account, to speak also of the new covenant, in order to draw from it that which concerns us so directly; for in the new covenant the magical healing of diseases was wrought in so many instances without visible means, as well by Christ himself as by the apostles, that it would seem as though all these miracles and all these cures had been nothing else than the fruit of magic or of magnetism. There are here certain extreme positions, maintained as much by the defenders as by the opponents of these miracles, which we shall notice more particularly in this place. In concluding this section, we shall cast a glance upon the existence and significance of Christianity in general, as well as upon the relation which it bears to magic.

The men of God of the old covenant, who wrought such great wonders and accomplished such marvellous works, always stood rather on the side of humanity than on the side of the divine; that is to say, they never manifested more than isolated powers and perfections. The universal expression of full perfection became an absolute reality only through Christ. It was he who first opened the new door, broke the chains of bondage, and showed men the true image of perfection and of wisdom in all their fulness. Christ restored to humanity the assurance of immortality. He raised the spiritual being to the rank of a temple of the sacred fire, and made of it a living altar and an incense for an everlasting peace.

“Since the first man, Adam,” we read in the *Magikon*, “is the first source of evil, none of his descendants could be the Saviour, for weakness cannot rule over strength; to be the Saviour, a being was required who was more than a man. Since God alone is superior to man, this agent, or Saviour, could be nothing less than the essential idea of the divine powers. In order to awaken in the soul of man the consciousness of what God really was, he had to bear within himself the divine character. The very judgments, so diverse, which men have passed upon Christ show conclusively that all power, all gifts and all perfections were united in him.”

There are beings for whom the Redeemer has already come, others for whom he is coming now, and others still for whom he is yet to come. Since his advent all things have become simpler, and He himself will become more and more simple, until all that is earthly vanishes away. A great sabbath of universal love and peace, such as it was in the creation, will mark its end. He entered into the Holy of Holies as the true High Priest, and restored to the elect, through his spirit, not only the lost words of the old book, but he gave them a new one, richer in content, for the healing of every evil and to render them invulnerable. He gave them, moreover, the holy incense of prayer, and showed them that without it they would be unable — save through Him alone — to obtain any principle of life. He accomplished upon earth that which is found above. He was constantly active, as the highest incarnation of wisdom, in works of charity spiritual and temporal, and he united the two into one. But this could only be because he was himself united, upon earth, in that unity in which he had been united from all eternity. At the last he crowned his work by conferring a spirit which created a knowledge and a vitality that had never been experienced before. He chose a sensible object as the channel for communicating the highest powers of life. Even man can transfer his feeble powers to any object whatsoever: how much more must the mysteries instituted by Himself (baptism by water, communion by bread and wine) possess a power which man could never possess! The action of the Holy Communion is at once corporeal, spiritual and divine, and all the things which are contained therein must become spirit and life, because He himself who instituted it was spirit and life.

Every true Christian is a living expression of this doctrine and an image of its author. He possesses fervour enough to absorb all that is sick and dissolute, and his life is a daily offering, in humility and holy fear before God, for the mysteries of God are revealed only to those who fear Him. The true Christian rests upon the commandments of the author of his name. Only such a man can enter into the counsel of peace. If the highest human wisdom remains a tottering and perishable edifice, a single ray of the sun of the world will render him purer and wiser than all the wise men of this earth. Since there are mysteries in every religion, there are likewise, in Christianity, certain things of indescribable power and of the highest weight, which cannot be explained. So long as they were known only to their true possessors, as a sanctuary, Christianity was at rest. But as soon as the great ones of the earth began to set foot within this sanctuary and wished to see with unprepared eyes, as soon as it was converted into a political machine, divisions and uncertainties followed. Thereupon came the high priests, who withdrew further and further from the original purity, and there resulted in this way a misshapen mixture, a veritable monstrosity. The sophists, who sprang up like weeds, multiplied these evils by their subtleties, separating what was united, and covering with darkness and death that which formerly was light and life. If a few traces of purity, zeal and strength could still be perceived here and there, they could accomplish nothing, for the horrors of desolation had already become too general and too many people preferred them. These corruptions were the cause, in later times, that the edifice of Christianity was undermined in its very foundations. It is but a step from deism to total ruin. From deism sprang a still worse brood, that of the materialists, who declared that every bond of humanity with higher powers was nothing but vain imagination, and who did not even believe in their own existence. It was very rare that the generations of the earliest ages sinned by great undertakings; those of later epochs, on the contrary, sinned by nullity. But there is one truth whose holiness cannot be shaken, and which will remain firm so long as the world exists.

Now, if man, through his reconciliation and return to God, and through a truly Christian life, receives the powers which the Saviour promised to his disciples, namely: “to cast out serpents, to heal the sick and to expel demons,” and that in the same measure as he did himself (John 14, 12); and if such a Christian can, in fact and in truth, perform greater wonders than he who lives in the state of sin (and we find that it is so not only with the apostles, but with all pious men of all ages), then we must grant to man that which is human. I have already spoken of the Christian method of healing, and since I refer to it in this place, the fact can admit of no doubt: the cures wrought by Christ, as well as by the apostles, really pertained to magic and magnetism. Never did they obtain from the apothecary the means of healing diseases, nor did they possess any secret remedies or magical essences; they possessed an inherent power of healing diseases: by words they cast out demons, restored the dead to life, healed, by prayer and the laying on of hands, the lame and the paralytic, made the blind to see and the dumb to speak. To prove this — not indeed to represent them solely as magnetic cures, but to examine them as humanly divine wonders — I shall mention a few cures wrought by Christ and his apostles, as they are recorded by the Evangelists and in the Acts of the Apostles:

“When he was come down from the mountain, great multitudes followed him. And, behold, there came a leper and worshipped him, saying, Lord, if thou wilt, thou canst make me clean. And Jesus put forth his hand, and touched him, saying, I will; be thou clean. And immediately his leprosy was cleansed.” (Matthew 8, 1-3.)

“And when Jesus was entered into Capernaum, there came unto him a centurion, beseeching him to heal his servant. And Jesus saith unto him, I will come and heal him. The centurion answered and said, Lord, I am not worthy that thou shouldest come under my roof: but speak the word only, and my servant shall be healed. When Jesus heard it, he marvelled, and said to them that followed, Verily I say unto you, I have not found so great faith, no, not in Israel. Go thy way; and as thou hast believed, so be it done unto thee. And his servant was healed in the selfsame hour.” (Matthew 8, 5-13.)

“And when Jesus was come into Peter’s house, he saw his wife’s mother laid, and sick of a fever. And he touched her hand, and the fever left her: and she arose, and ministered unto them.” (Matthew 8, 14-15.)

“When the even was come, they brought unto him many that were possessed with devils: and he cast out the spirits with his word, and healed all that were sick.” (Matthew 8, 16; Mark 1, 32.)

“And, behold, they brought to him a man sick of the palsy, lying on a bed: and Jesus seeing their faith said unto the sick of the palsy, Son, be of good cheer; thy sins be forgiven thee.” (Matthew 9, 2; Mark 2, 3.)

“And, behold, a woman, which was diseased with an issue of blood twelve years, came behind him, and touched the hem of his garment: for she said within herself, If I may but touch his garment, I shall be whole. But Jesus turned him about, and when he saw her, he said, Daughter, be of good comfort; thy faith hath made thee whole. And the woman was made whole from that hour.” (Matthew 9, 20-22.)

“And when Jesus came into the ruler’s house, and saw the minstrels and the people making a noise, he said unto them, Give place: for the maid is not dead, but sleepeth. And they laughed him to scorn. But when the people were put forth, he went in, and took her by the hand, and the maid arose.”

“And when Jesus departed thence, two blind men followed him, etc. And when he was come into the house, the blind men came to him: and Jesus saith unto them, Believe ye that I am able to do this? They said unto him, Yea, Lord. Then touched he their eyes, saying, According to your faith be it unto you. And their eyes were opened.” (Matthew 9, 27-30.)

The man with the withered hand he healed with these words: “Stretch forth thine hand: and it was restored whole, like as the other.” (Matthew 12, 10-13.)

“And when the men of that place had knowledge of him, they sent out into all that country round about, and brought unto him all that were diseased; and besought him that they might only touch the hem of his garment: and as many as touched were made perfectly whole.” (Matthew 14, 35-36.)

The daughter of the woman of Canaan, who was grievously vexed with a devil, was delivered through the faith of her mother. (Matthew 15, 22-28.)

“And great multitudes came unto him, having with them those that were lame, blind, dumb, maimed, and many others, and cast them down at Jesus’ feet; and he healed them.” (Matthew 15, 30; Luke 7, 22.)

The lunatic who ofttimes fell into the fire and into the water could not be healed by the disciples. But when Jesus had rebuked the devil, it departed out of him, and the child was cured from that very hour. Jesus said to his disciples: “Because of your unbelief ye could not heal him. For verily I say unto you, If ye have faith as a grain of mustard seed, ye shall say unto this mountain, Remove hence to yonder place; and it shall remove. Howbeit this kind goeth not out but by prayer and fasting.” (Matthew 17, 14-21.)

Two blind men, on the road to Jericho, cried out to the Son of David to come to their aid: “Then touched he their eyes, saying, According to your faith be it unto you. And their eyes were opened.” (Matthew 9, 27-36 *[sic; the Jericho episode is in Matthew 20, 30-34]*.)

He healed the lame and the blind in the temple at Jerusalem, who came to him to be healed. (Matthew 21, 14.)

He healed the man in the synagogue, who had a spirit of an unclean devil, and whom he rebuked, saying: “Hold thy peace, and come out of him.” (Luke 4, 33.)

“Now when the sun was setting, all they that had any sick with divers diseases brought them unto him; and he laid his hands on every one of them, and healed them.” (Luke 4, 40.)

“Now when he came nigh to the gate of the city (Nain), behold, there was a dead man carried out, the only son of his mother. And when the Lord saw her, he had compassion on her, and said unto her, Weep not. And he came and touched the bier. And he said, Young man, I say unto thee, Arise. And he that was dead sat up, and began to speak. And he delivered him to his mother.” (Luke 7, 12-15.)

Mary, called Magdalene, out of whom went seven devils, the wife of Chuza, and many others possessed of evil spirits, were healed and delivered. (Luke 8, 2-3.)

The man possessed of a legion of devils, which were cast out into a herd of swine. (Luke 8, 27-33.)

“And, behold, there was a woman which had a spirit of infirmity eighteen years, and was bowed together, and could in no wise lift up herself. And when Jesus saw her, he called her to him, and said unto her, Woman, thou art loosed from thine infirmity. And he laid his hands on her: and immediately she was made straight, and glorified God.” (Luke 13, 11-13.)

He healed at Capernaum the son of the king’s officer, who was at the point of death, through the faith of the child’s father. (John 4, 47.)

The man who had been infirm for thirty-eight years, whom no one would carry into the pool by the sheep market, whose waters, when they were troubled by the angel, healed every disease, he healed with these words: “Rise, take up thy bed, and walk.” (John 5, 2-8.)

The man born blind he healed with moistened earth and spittle: “He spat on the ground, and made clay of the spittle, and he anointed the eyes of the blind man with the clay, and said unto him, Go, wash in the pool of Siloam. He went his way therefore, and washed, and came seeing.” (John 9, 1-7.)

Saint Mark relates a still more remarkable cure, wrought by Christ upon a blind man: “And he cometh to Bethsaida; and they bring a blind man unto him, and besought him to touch him. And he took the blind man by the hand, and led him out of the town; and when he had spit on his eyes, and put his hands upon him, he asked him if he saw ought. And he looked up, and said, I see men as trees, walking. After that he put his hands again upon his eyes, and made him look up: and he was restored, and saw every man clearly.” (Mark 8, 22-25.)

He raised Lazarus from the dead by a fervent prayer to the Father. “Then when Jesus came, he found that he had lain in the grave four days already. It was a cave, and a stone lay upon it. Jesus said, Take ye away the stone. Martha, the sister of him that was dead, saith unto him, Lord, by this time he stinketh: for he hath been dead four days. Jesus saith unto her, Said I not unto thee, that, if thou wouldest believe, thou shouldest see the glory of God? Then they took away the stone from the place where the dead was laid. And Jesus lifted up his eyes, and said, Father, I thank thee that thou hast heard me. And when he thus had spoken, he cried with a loud voice, Lazarus, come forth! And he that was dead came forth, bound hand and foot with graveclothes: and his face was bound about with a napkin. Jesus saith unto them, Loose him, and let him go.” (John 11.)

Such are a few of the cures wrought by our Saviour and recorded by the Evangelists. There exist other legendary accounts of wonders performed by Christ, which the Evangelists have not recorded, and of which I shall mention only one. It is found in the *History of Jesus* by Eusebius (chap. 12, p. 16), whence Büsching took it for his “Weekly Sheet of Researches into the History, Magic and Learning of the Middle Ages” (vol. 2, p. 64, Dec. 1817, Breslau). It is printed also in Kosegarden’s *Legends*. As an extraordinary example of the calling and power of Christ, of his divine mission and of his love for mankind cannot fail to interest the friends of Christianity, we shall give the contents of several letters written by Abgarus, king of Edessa, who lived in the very time of Jesus.

The extraordinary mission of Christ and the fame of the wonders he wrought had spread even to the most distant countries, in particular his divine power of healing all manner of diseases. These rumours attracted also the attention of Abgarus, who was the victim of a grievous disease. He therefore wrote to Jesus the following letter:

“Abgarus, king of Edessa, to Jesus, the compassionate Saviour, who hath appeared in the flesh in the neighbourhood of Jerusalem — Greeting!

“I have heard of Thy sublime virtues and of the marvellous cures which Thou performest without the use of medicines or herbs. It is reported that Thou makest the blind to see, the lame to walk, and that Thou cleansest the lepers; that Thou castest out unclean spirits, that Thou healest those who are afflicted with cruel diseases, and that Thou recallest the dead to life. On learning these things concerning Thee, I have formed two opinions: either Thou art God, descended from the heights of Heaven, or Thou art the Son of God, from whom proceed all these marvellous works. Wherefore I write Thee this, fervently praying Thee to be pleased to take the trouble of healing me of the grievous disease with which I am chastised. I am told that the Jews threaten Thee with great evils and resist Thee with anger. It is true that I possess but a small city, but it is well provided, fortified and renowned, so that it will afford us all that is necessary.”

Thus wrote Abgarus to Christ. Illumined with a divine light, this short letter contains that which is of true value, being full of power and virtue. The following reply was sent by Jesus to King Abgarus, by the hands of Ananias, who was chosen as messenger:

“Abgarus! Blessed art thou, who hast not seen and yet hast believed in me. For it is written that they who see me believe not in me, in order that they who see me not may believe in me and be saved. As to that which thou writest to me, I must first accomplish the things for which I have been sent into the world; after I shall have accomplished them all, I shall be taken up again to him who sent me. As soon as I shall have ascended, I will send thee one of my disciples, who will heal thee of thy painful disease, so that thy life and the lives of thine may be preserved.”

After the ascension of Christ into heaven, Judas, called also Thaddeus, one of the seventy disciples, was sent to Abgarus; he halted for the night, in the course of his journey, in the house of Tobias, son of Tobit. Abgarus learned that the disciple whom Jesus had promised to send him had arrived, for Tobias had reported to him that he had lodged a mighty man come from Jerusalem, who healed many diseases in the name of Jesus. “Bring him to me,” said Abgarus. Tobias went at once to Thaddeus and said to him: “King Abgarus sends me to thee, and he desires that I lead thee to him, that thou mayest remove the disease which torments him.” “Let us go,” answered Thaddeus, “for it is on his account that I have been sent hither.” The next morning Tobias took Thaddeus with him, and they repaired to Abgarus. On his arrival, the lords of the castle stood ready to defend Abgarus; but when the disciple entered, Abgarus perceived a dazzling light upon his face. On seeing it, Abgarus prostrated himself humbly before Thaddeus. The astonishment of all present was complete, for they could not see the strange apparition which Abgarus saw. “Thou art truly the disciple of Jesus, the Son of God, who promised me in his letter, saying: I will send thee one of my disciples, who will heal thine infirmity and give life to thee and to those who belong to thee!” Whereupon Thaddeus answered: “I have been sent to thee because of thy trust in Jesus, who hath sent me.” And he added: “If the faith which thou hast in him grows more and more, then the desire of thy heart shall be fulfilled according to thy faith.” Abgarus answered: “I have faith in Him to such a degree that, if the Roman power did not prevent me, I would destroy with my armies the Jews who crucified Him.” Thaddeus said: “The Lord our God, Jesus Christ, hath accomplished the will of his Father, and when his will was accomplished, he was taken up to his Father.” Then Abgarus said: “I believe in Him and in his Father.” Thaddeus answered: “Therefore I lay my hands upon thee, in the name of Jesus”; and as he did so, Abgarus was healed from that very hour.

To this is attached the tradition relating to the sending of the portrait of Christ to Abgarus. According to certain authors, chiefly the Damascenes, Abgarus sent a painter to Jerusalem to make the portrait of Jesus; but the painter could not finish his portrait, on account of the splendour of his countenance. Then the Lord impressed, by divine power, his likeness upon his mantle, and in this manner satisfied the desire of Abgarus. According to others, Jesus impressed his features upon a linen cloth and sent it to Abgarus. Whatever may be the truth as to the completion and sending of this portrait, the Damascenes show, by later circumstances, that such a portrait was found at Edessa at one time or another; otherwise we should be compelled to reckon the account of the siege of the city of Abgarus by the Persian king Chosroes among the inventions which cannot be authenticated. According to this legend, the walls of Edessa, built of olive wood, were surrounded by Chosroes with a pyre of poplar wood, in order to burn them. The king of Edessa then reigning, whose name is not given, had recourse to the linen shroud upon which the features of Christ were impressed and which had been sent to Abgarus. Whereupon (*divina vi* [by a divine force]) there arose a terrible whirlwind, which drove the flames away from the city and set the pyre of poplar wood ablaze, consuming everything that was in its immediate vicinity.

That the promises concerning Christ — namely, that he would heal the sick — were fulfilled, the proof is given by the additional wonders which the apostles wrought, who healed diseases in the same manner as their Master:

“And a certain man lame from his mother’s womb was carried, whom they laid daily at the gate of the temple which is called Beautiful, to ask alms of them that entered into the temple; who seeing Peter and John about to go into the temple asked an alms. And Peter, fastening his eyes upon him with John, said, Look on us. And he gave heed unto them, expecting to receive something of them. Then Peter said, Silver and gold have I none; but such as I have give I thee: In the name of Jesus Christ of Nazareth rise up and walk. And he took him by the right hand, and lifted him up: and immediately his feet and ankle bones received strength, and he leaping up stood, praising God.” (Acts 3, 2-8.)

“And believers were the more added to the Lord, multitudes both of men and women, insomuch that they brought forth the sick into the streets, and laid them on beds and couches, that at the least the shadow of Peter passing by might overshadow some of them. There came also a multitude out of the cities round about unto Jerusalem, bringing sick folks, and them which were vexed with unclean spirits: and they were healed every one.” (Acts 5, 14-16.)

The history of Simon the magician is particularly remarkable, he who, on account of his knavery, could not perform these wonders, and who attempted to purchase from the apostles, for money, the gift of working them. This marvellous history applies also to the Simons of our own day. “But there was a certain man, called Simon, which beforetime in the same city used sorcery (magic), and who was converted under the preaching of Philip and baptized. And the apostles gave the Holy Ghost to the new converts. They laid their hands on them, and they received the Holy Ghost. And when Simon saw that through laying on of the apostles’ hands the Holy Ghost was given, he offered them money, saying, Give me also this power, that on whomsoever I lay hands, he may receive the Holy Ghost. But Peter said unto him, Thy money perish with thee, because thou hast thought that the gift of God may be purchased with money! Thou hast neither part nor lot in this matter: for thy heart is not right in the sight of God. Repent therefore of this thy wickedness, and pray God, if perhaps the thought of thine heart may be forgiven thee. For I perceive that thou art in the gall of bitterness, and in the bond of iniquity.” (Acts 8, 9-24.) See also the history of Aeneas, who had kept his bed eight years and was sick of the palsy. (Acts 9, 33-34; 38-41.)

Of the same nature also are the marvellous works of Paul, who excites our admiration by his strength of spirit and his power in all things. “And there sat a certain man at Lystra, impotent in his feet, being a cripple from his mother’s womb, who never had walked. The same heard Paul speak: who stedfastly beholding him, and perceiving that he had faith to be healed, said with a loud voice, Stand upright on thy feet. And he leaped and walked.” (Acts 14, 8-10.)

“And God wrought special miracles by the hands of Paul, so that from his body were brought unto the sick handkerchiefs or aprons, and the diseases departed from them, and the evil spirits went out of them.” (Acts 19, 11-12.)

The young man named Eutychus, who fell down from the third loft and was taken up dead, was recalled to life by Paul in the following manner: “And Paul went down, and fell on him, and embracing him said, Trouble not yourselves; for his life is in him. When he therefore was come up again, he broke bread and ate, and talked a long while, even till break of day, and so he departed. And they brought the young man alive, and were not a little comforted.” (Acts 20, 8-12.) “And it came to pass, that the father of Publius lay sick of a fever and of a bloody flux: to whom Paul entered in, and prayed, and laid his hands on him, and healed him. So when this was done, others also, which had diseases in the island, came, and were healed.” (Acts 28, 8-9.)

Having already spoken of the scope of Christian healing and given upon this subject indications of a more or less historical character, I shall leave it to each reader to draw his own conclusions as to their particulars, so that he may retain from them that which is most instructive. One thing must not be omitted in conclusion: it is that one must first become a Christian before being able to work cures according to Christian methods. Very few are really Christians among those who give themselves that name; they are Christians only in name and in appearance.

The art of healing according to scriptural principles deserves special mention in this place, on more than one account: not only because something truly magical takes place therein, but because scriptural healing is carefully established upon certain declarations and doctrines of the Bible. (See Leviticus 26, 14; Deuteronomy 28, 15-22, etc.; Exodus 14, 26 *[sic; read Exodus 15, 26]*; as well as Ecclesiasticus 38, 9; Psalms 107, 17-20.)

There exists, therefore, in this manner, a medical science higher than the ordinary science, and pious persons other than physicians can heal diseases. “The believing physician,” says Macarius, “is like unto God; but the medical body belongs to the heathen and the unbelievers.” Nevertheless, according to the wise saying of Sirach, the physician is entitled to honour, for he says: “Honour a physician with the honour due unto him for the uses which ye may have of him: for the Lord hath created him, and he shall receive honour of the king. The skill of the physician shall lift up his head: and in the sight of great men he shall be in admiration.” But he too believes that the physician is made only for the sinner: “He that sinneth before his Maker, let him fall into the hand of the physician.” (Sirach 38, 15.)

In the New Testament also, diseases are generally attributed to sin. Jesus said to the paralytic in healing him: “Thy sins be forgiven thee,” and he was healed. And when he had healed the man at the pool of Bethesda, who had been infirm for thirty-eight years, meeting him afterwards in the temple, he said to him: “Behold, thou art made whole: sin no more, lest a worse thing come unto thee.” (John 5, 14.) The apostles also, and all the saints, insisted upon first healing the sick man morally, for a true restoration of the diseased body and spirit can be effected only by a return to God. It is truly remarkable that the sages of the East, Zoroaster and all the defenders of the doctrines of emission (the system of emanation), the kabbalists as well as all the later theosophists — all of whom possessed an extraordinary power of healing diseases — should have maintained this doctrine. Some of them impute diseases directly to sin, while others attribute them to evil spirits, with whom man associates himself through sin. That evil spirits are the cause of diseases, destroying and vitiating the healthy sap of the organism, no one, even of ideas less spiritual than those of the theosophists, will deny: these are the true bonds which Satan hath bound. (Luke 13, 16.)

The originally pure doctrine of Christianity was, however, worked upon from the earliest times by the defenders of the system of emanations, which was, through the distortions introduced into it by nominal Christians, far more destructive than had been intended. Saturninus, Basilides and Carpocrates stood at the head of those according to whose theory everything proceeded from the Aeon (celestial powers). Christ himself was for them an Aeon of the first rank, who, by a rigorous abstention from sensualism, subdued the demons (evil spirits); and he who lives as Christ lived can subdue them likewise. “From the Aeon, the first source,” says Basilides, “heaven was brought into existence.” According to Valentinus, one of the most celebrated doctors of that time, the Aeons were divided into classes, and even into male and female classes. Thus, the principal female Aeon was the Holy Ghost. By the laying on of consecrated hands, the subject was made the recipient of this Aeon and sent forth to heal demoniacal diseases. Although this digression created a variety of ideas departing from the original doctrine, the effort to heal diseases according to scriptural principles remained, for a very long time, a dominant struggle for moral improvement and perfection. If a being applies himself earnestly to living in unconditional obedience to God, and is converted to God by a living and active faith, then God becomes his physician, and he has no further need of the services of an earthly doctor. As soon as the soul is in a state of perfect health, it is said, this health communicates itself to the body; or rather, the sufferings of the body can no longer be regarded as disease — these sufferings cannot reach the soul which has been raised to heaven. But if a man is not capable of such a healing by himself, let him then turn to the physician, for “God hath created him, and he hath given men skill, that he might be honoured in his marvellous works.” (Sirach 38, 6.) The particular manner in which the physician must heal according to scriptural principles follows from what precedes. He must, in the first place, become a truly Christian physician, that is to say, a physician-priest. He can succour the sick only through his own health, above all through the health of his own soul, and only when he is himself pure. He must heal the inner man (the soul), for without the repose of the soul (inner peace) there can be no true healing of the body; it is therefore indispensable that a true physician be also a true priest.

The question whether the scriptural physician is above the necessity of employing medicines, or whether he may sometimes resort to their use for healing diseases, may also easily receive a scriptural answer, and it presents itself in the same terms as the question whether the magnetic physician has need of medicines. As a general rule, he must possess, as a scriptural physician, and more especially as a Christian physician, the power of healing diseases by prayer and the divine word, without medicines; and it is only in certain cases, and when he does not himself possess this power in sufficient measure, that he may resort to the use of medicines. They are not made in vain. “For of the most High cometh healing.” “The Lord hath created medicines out of the earth; and he that is wise will not abhor them. With such doth he heal men, and taketh away their pains.” (Sirach 38, 4, 7.) Medicines are good, but they rarely suffice. “Go up into Gilead, and take balm, saith Jeremiah; in vain shalt thou use many medicines; for thou shalt not be cured.” (Jeremiah 46, 11.) There are, however, also in the Bible examples where, on rare occasions, recourse was had to physical remedies. Moses, by casting wood into the waters of Marah, made them sweet. (Exodus 15, 25.) His cure of leprosy, by washings, purifications, etc., was truly remarkable. Elijah cast salt into the bitter spring, and it became wholesome for ever. He also cast meal into the pot in which was death, and the herbs became harmless. Isaiah laid figs upon the glands of King Hezekiah and healed him. Tobias healed his blind father with the gall of a fish, a cure which was indicated to him by an angel. And Jesus himself anointed the eyes of the blind man with spittle and clay, and told him to wash in the pool of Siloam, etc. For the rest, according to the Bible, only external remedies were employed in cures, and those of the simplest and least elaborate kind. No internal remedies were employed. The means of healing consisted in spiritual purification, in conversion from sin, in prayer to the Father of Life — the physician of the believer. Thus we read in James 5, 13-16: “Is any among you afflicted? let him pray. Is any merry? let him sing psalms. Is any sick among you? let him call for the elders of the church; and let them pray over him, anointing him with oil in the name of the Lord. And the prayer of faith shall save the sick, and the Lord shall raise him up; and if he have committed sins, they shall be forgiven him. Confess your faults one to another, and pray one for another, that ye may be healed. The effectual fervent prayer of a righteous man availeth much.” In Saint Mark, 6, 12, we read: “And they went out, and preached that men should repent. And they cast out devils, and anointed with oil many that were sick, and healed them.”

But the scriptural physician does not always heal, and disease is not always an evil. If temporal enjoyment and earthly felicity were the destiny and end of man, towards which the great majority tend, then we should be justified in regarding disease as a great misfortune and a heavy chastisement, which many will not admit to have deserved. But this planet is not a place of undisturbed peace — it is not the abode of beings called to rejoice in a final happiness. Light and shadow, day and night, rest and activity, love and hatred, peace and war, joy and sorrow, fortune and misfortune, health and sickness, life and death: such are the constant vicissitudes of this world, and they are not due to chance, but disposed with foresight by a higher hand, in order to serve as a touchstone, so that, through affliction and suffering, by overcoming evil and purifying ourselves from sensualism, we may prepare ourselves for a better existence. The principal object is the health of the soul and of the spirit; the health of the body is a secondary matter. If the soul is in a state of health, the body will become healthy as a consequence. “His flesh shall be fresher than a child’s: he shall return to the days of his youth.” (Job 33, 25.) And if so strong a soul should not recover its health, it will not feel bodily suffering, because it already enjoys a foretaste of the blessedness of the other world. True happiness and perfect rest cannot be found upon earth; let us therefore not be astonished, but neither let us weep, at the fleetingness of tranquil and unmingled pleasure — of the purest love, because it is so soon darkened by gloomy clouds and driven away by furious tempests — and let us not grieve that true happiness can be found only in another world, that true health exists only in the souls of those who are truly united to God, and that help, consolation and blessing come only from the Father who is in heaven.

*End of the first volume.*

# PART II — MAGICAL KABBALAH AND MAGICAL LAWS OF MOSES

## Magical Kabbalah

**MAGICAL KABBALAH, or the MAGICAL ART of the SIXTH AND SEVENTH BOOKS OF MOSES, together with an EXTRACT from the true and genuine KABBALAH OF SOLOMON, KING OF ISRAEL.**

*Weimar, 1595.*

### Note — To the Friends of the Magical Kabbalah

The formulas of citation contained in this book must be pronounced only in the Hebrew language, and in no other. In any other language they have no power whatever, and the Master can never be assured of his cause. For all these words and forms were thus pronounced by the Great Spirit, and have power only in the Hebrew language.

## Extract from the Magical Kabbalah of the Sixth and Seventh Books of Moses

### Breastplate of Moses

*(Hebrew characters — see fig.)*

These Hebrew words are pronounced as follows:

**Jehova, aser Ebeje Cether Eleion Eheje.**

The Most High, whom no eye hath seen, of whom no tongue hath spoken; the Spirit that accomplished great deeds and wrought great wonders.

This Breastplate and this Helmet, when pronounced, signify Holiness.

### Helmet of Moses and Aaron

**Bietol mare aotitas hame donene ariaeroh.**

These are the names which the ancient Egyptians used in place of the ineffable name of Asser Oriel; they are called “the Fire of God” and “Strong Rock of Faith.” Whoever carries them upon his person, engraved upon a plate of gold, shall not die a sudden death.

### Breastplate of Aaron

**Sadajai amara elon hejiana vananel phenatori ebeoel tottfal.**

That is to say: one a Prince of Mine, the other leads to Jehova *[sic — text corrupt in the original]*. Thus this God spoke to Moses.

*(Hebrew characters — see fig.)*

**Aila himel adonay amara Zebaoth cadas yeseraije haralios.**

These words are terrible; they will assemble the demons or the spirits, or else they will cause the dead to appear.

These words are great and mighty. They are the names of the Creator and the characters inscribed upon the Ark of the Covenant.

*(Hebrew characters — see fig.)*

**Uniel dilatan Sadai paneim usamigaras caliphos sasna panoi soim.**

These names, if anyone desires to accomplish something by the four elements or by anything else pertaining thereto, will prove efficacious; but they cannot be translated into the vulgar tongue.

**Leay yli Ziarite zelobabe et negoramy Zien latebm damn mecha timeti ozira.**

By this dismissal, all things dissolve into nothing.

**Malcoh, Sadaij, cubor Damabiah menkie lejabel maniah ijejavai.**

That is to say: Strong and mighty Spirit of hell, return into thy works, in the name of Jehova.

### Sorcery of Balaam

**Meloch, hel alokim tiphret hod jesath.**

This draws down vengeance upon enemies, and must not be held a small thing, for it contains the names of the Seven Tables of the Ark of the Covenant.

### Egypt

**Tanabtain ainaten pagnij aijolo asnia hicbaifale matae habonr hijcero.**

By these words Moses spoke to the sorcerers in Egypt. They signify: “the Lord appeared to his servant in the fire, to seal the earth in its four quarters, and the lower earth.”

**Aijcon dunsanas pethanir thrijgnir ijon cijna naterlavis pistoin.**

If you would pronounce these words, you must fast three days, and by them you will be able to accomplish wonders. They cannot be translated, on account of the Hebrew characters.

This citation is great and mighty: these are the names of the Creator, and the names of the two Cherubim of the Mercy-Seat, Zarall and Jael.

Such are the high and mighty words which Moses employed for the awakening of Leviathan, in order to compel him to serve his Lord. The first cannot be uttered, and the first inhabitants of the earth used it as a mighty Lord. The whole is good, but not all can obtain it in its perfection without a severe discipline.

## Principal Citation upon All the Ministering Spirits of the Air and of the Earth

Principal Citation upon all the ministering spirits of the air and of the earth, as Manasseh and Solomon employed it as the true Key — *Clavicula Salomonis Regis Israel* [the Clavicle of Solomon, King of Israel].

### Extract from the True Clavicula of Solomon and from the Girdle of Aaron

Bequeathed by testament to all wise Magicians, which all the ancient Fathers possessed and employed, in order to obtain and accomplish all things by the illustrious power of the almighty God Jehovah, as He, the great Monarch, gave it to His creatures who adore Him day and night with reverence and in fear, who loudly invoke His name in secret and sigh toward Him as toward their origin — reasonable beings existing of Him and through Him, on the point of being encompassed by the pains of the elements, and who aspire toward the supreme Being, toward God and with God. To these He has given this: to those who will not forget Him amid the pleasures of this world; who, still bearing suffering, forget neither reality nor the perishable glitter of the world.

You must stand upright upon a prominent rock, hold a palm branch in the right hand, and wear a crown of laurel about the temples. Then turn toward the East and say:

**Alija Laija Laumin Otheon!**

Whereupon a halo of light will surround you, and when you perceive this light, fall upon your knees and adore. Then say in an audible voice, slowly and distinctly:
